[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.


Tool session rules:
- You have exactly one tool: `python`.
- When calling `python`, emit exactly the tool-call format shown elsewhere in this prompt for this model. Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. Do not quote or place tool-call markup inside explanatory text; when you decide to call the tool, emit the tool call itself.
- Your workbench persists: every function you define stays defined for the rest of this game, so build a small library of your own helpers (parsers, coordinate maps, rule checkers) instead of rewriting them each turn. Imports and other variables do not carry over--only functions.
- `notes` is a string that persists across turns and is shown back to you each turn. Append a line to it whenever you confirm a rule (`notes = notes + "\n- confirmed: clicking a tile advances its base color"`). Keep it to confirmed mechanics and the current plan.
- `action(...)` refreshes runtime state immediately. Inspection-only calls preserve both `last_action_result` and `last_animation`.
- Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

# Extract B patterns (5x5 interior) from each 7x7 top object
# Top objects at rows 4-10, 13-19, 22-28, cols 12-18, 22-28, 35-41, 45-51
# Interior is rows+1 to rows+5, cols+1 to cols+5

def get_pattern(ascii_lines, r0, c0):
    """Extract 5x5 interior pattern from 7x7 object starting at (r0,c0)"""
    pattern = []
    for r in range(r0+1, r0+6):
        row = ''
        for c in range(c0+1, c0+6):
            row += ascii_lines[r][c]
        pattern.append(row)
    return pattern

# Top patterns
top_objects = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [12, 22, 35, 45]:
        p = get_pattern(ascii_lines, r0, c0)
        top_objects.append((r0, c0, p))
        print(f"Top obj at ({r0},{c0}):")
        for line in p:
            print("  ", line)
        print()

# Bottom S pattern - rows 41-45 (interior of rows 40-46), cols 15-49
print("Bottom S interior:")
for r in range(41, 46):

print()

    row = ''

prev = previous_frame

print("Step:", curr.step, "Level:", curr.level)

# Compare ascii to find changed cells

curr_lines = curr.ascii.split('\n')

changed = []
for r in range(64):
    for c in range(64):
        if prev_lines[r][c] != curr_lines[r][c]:
            changed.append((r, c, prev_lines[r][c], curr_lines[r][c]))

import numpy as np

# Define top P patterns
top_p = {
    'p1': ['BBBBP','BPPBB','BPPPB','BBPPB','PBBBB'],
    'p2': ['BBBBP','BPPBP','BPPBB','BPPBP','BBBBP'],
    'p3': ['BBBBB','BPPBP','BPPBP','BBBBP','BPPPP'],
    'p4': ['PPBBB','PPBPB','BBBBB','BPBPP','BBBPP'],
    'p5': ['BBBBB','BPPPB','BPBBB','BPBPB','BBBBB'],
    'p6': ['PPBPP','BBBBB','BPBPB','BBBBB','PPBPP'],
}

# Bottom P block 0 original and current
orig_block = ['BBBBB','PBPPB','PBPPB','PBBBB','PPPPB']
new_block = ['BBBBB','BPBPB','BPBBB','BPPPB','BBBBB']

def to_np(pattern):
    return np.array([[1 if c == 'B' else 0 for c in row] for row in pattern])

def rotate_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np(p), k=-1).astype(int).astype(str).tolist()]

# Actually let me use char arrays properly
def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])

def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]

def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]

def flip_h(p):
    return [row[::-1] for row in p]

def flip_v(p):
    return p[::-1]

# Check if new_block matches any rotation/reflection of top P patterns
for name, p in top_p.items():
    variants = {
        'orig': p,
        'rot90': rot_cw(p),
        'rot180': rot_cw(rot_cw(p)),
        'rot270': rot_cw(rot_cw(rot_cw(p))),
        'flip_h': flip_h(p),
        'flip_v': flip_v(p),
        'flip_h_rot90': rot_cw(flip_h(p)),
    }
    for vname, v in variants.items():
        if v == new_block:
            print(f"New block matches {name} {vname}!")
        if v == orig_block:
            print(f"Orig block matches {name} {vname}!")

# Also check if orig_block matches any top P pattern
print("\nChecking orig_block against top P:")
for name, p in top_p.items():
    if p == orig_block:
        print(f"  orig matches {name} directly")

# Print all top P patterns and the new block side by side
print("\nAll top P patterns:")
for name, p in top_p.items():
    print(f"{name}:")
    for row in p:
        print(f"  {row}")
print(f"\nNew block:")
for row in new_block:
    print(f"  {row}")
</parameter>
</function>

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])

def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]

def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]

def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

def flip_h(p):
    return [row[::-1] for row in p]

def flip_v(p):
    return p[::-1]

# Extract all top patterns
top_s = []
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [12, 22, 35, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        border_color = obj[0][0]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        if border_color == 'S':
            top_s.append({'pos': (r0, c0), 'pattern': interior})
        else:
            top_p.append({'pos': (r0, c0), 'pattern': interior})

# Extract bottom S blocks
bottom_s_blocks = []
for i in range(6):
    c0 = 15 + i*5
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(41, 46)]
    bottom_s_blocks.append(block)

# Extract bottom P blocks
bottom_p_blocks = []
for i in range(6):
    c0 = 15 + i*5
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(52, 57)]
    bottom_p_blocks.append(block)

# For each bottom S block, find which top S pattern it matches (with rotations/reflections)
print("Bottom S blocks matching top S patterns:")
for i, block in enumerate(bottom_s_blocks):
    matches = []
    for j, ts in enumerate(top_s):
        p = ts['pattern']
        variants = {
            'orig': p, 'rot90': rot_cw(p), 'rot180': rot_180(p), 'rot270': rot_ccw(p),
            'flip_h': flip_h(p), 'flip_v': flip_v(p),
        }
        for vname, v in variants.items():
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topS[{j}] at {ts['pos']} {vname}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

print()
print("Bottom P blocks matching top P patterns:")
for i, block in enumerate(bottom_p_blocks):
    matches = []
    for j, tp in enumerate(top_p):
        p = tp['pattern']
        variants = {
            'orig': p, 'rot90': rot_cw(p), 'rot180': rot_180(p), 'rot270': rot_ccw(p),
            'flip_h': flip_h(p), 'flip_v': flip_v(p),
        }
        for vname, v in variants.items():
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topP[{j}] at {tp['pos']} {vname}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])

def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]

def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]

def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Top S patterns
top_s = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [12, 35]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_s.append(interior)

# Check which top pattern each bottom block matches (any rotation)
print("Bottom P blocks → top P pattern matches:")
for i in range(6):
    c0 = 15 + i*5
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(52, 57)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

print("\nBottom S blocks → top S pattern matches:")
for i in range(6):
    c0 = 15 + i*5
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(41, 46)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_s):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topS[{j}] {name}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. - Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top S patterns
top_s = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [12, 35]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_s.append(interior)

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Current bottom S blocks
print("Bottom S blocks matching top S patterns:")
for i in range(6):
    c0 = 15 + i*5
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(41, 46)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_s):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topS[{j}] {name}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

# Current bottom P blocks
print("\nBottom P blocks matching top P patterns:")
for i in range(6):
    c0 = 15 + i*5
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(52, 57)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

# Print all top patterns for reference
print("\nTop S patterns:")
for i, p in enumerate(top_s):
    print(f"  S{i}:")
    for row in p:
        print(f"    {row}")
print("\nTop P patterns:")

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Original P block 1 (before DOWN)
orig_block1 = ['PBBBP','PBPBP','BBBBB','PBPBP','PBBBP']
print("Original P block 1 matches:")
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == orig_block1:
            print(f"  topP[{j}] {name}")

# New P block 1 (after DOWN)
new_block1 = ['BBBBB','BPPBP','BPPBP','BBBBP','BPPPP']
print("New P block 1 matches:")
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == new_block1:
            print(f"  topP[{j}] {name}")

# Let me also check what P block 0 was originally and what it became
# State 0 (initial): BBBBB / PBPPB / PBPPB / PBBBB / PPPPB → topP[2] rot90

# Block 1:

# Let me check what topP pattern the original block 1 matches
# Let me print all top P patterns and their rotations

for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    print(f"  topP[{j}] orig:")
    for row in p:

    print(f"  topP[{j}] rot90:")

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Current bottom P blocks
print("Current bottom P blocks and their top P matches:")
for i in range(6):
    c0 = 15 + i*5
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(52, 57)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")
    # Print the block
    for row in block:
        print(f"    {row}")
    print()

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Top S patterns
top_s = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [12, 35]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_s.append(interior)

# Bottom P blocks at positions 15, 22, 29, 36, 43
positions = [15, 22, 29, 36, 43]
print("Bottom P blocks matching top P patterns:")
for i, c0 in enumerate(positions):
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(52, 57)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
    print(f"  P Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")

print("\nBottom S blocks matching top S patterns:")
for i, c0 in enumerate(positions):
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(41, 46)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_s):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topS[{j}] {name}")

[SYSTEM PROMPT]
You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.


Tool session rules:
- You have exactly one tool: `python`.
- When calling `python`, emit exactly the tool-call format shown elsewhere in this prompt for this model. Use only that format; do not add markdown fences, prose wrappers, or alternate tool-call syntax. Do not quote or place tool-call markup inside explanatory text; when you decide to call the tool, emit the tool call itself.
- Your workbench persists: every function you define stays defined for the rest of this game, so build a small library of your own helpers (parsers, coordinate maps, rule checkers) instead of rewriting them each turn. Imports and other variables do not carry over--only functions.
- `notes` is a string that persists across turns and is shown back to you each turn. Append a line to it whenever you confirm a rule (`notes = notes + "\n- confirmed: clicking a tile advances its base color"`). Keep it to confirmed mechanics and the current plan.
- `action(...)` refreshes runtime state immediately. Inspection-only calls preserve both `last_action_result` and `last_animation`.
- Each call has a 30-second limit and about 1024 output tokens. Keep output compact; truncation is reported.

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')
w_pos = []
for r in [48, 49]:
    for c in range(14, 49):
        if ascii_lines[r][c] == 'W':
            w_pos.append((r, c))

# Now press DOWN to cycle P Block 3
action(['DOWN'])
print("\nAfter DOWN on block 3:", last_action_result.get('board_changed'))

# Check what changed
prev_lines = previous_frame.ascii.split('\n')
curr_lines = current_frame.ascii.split('\n')
changed = []
for r in range(64):
    for c in range(64):
        if prev_lines[r][c] != curr_lines[r][c]:
            changed.append((r, c, prev_lines[r][c], curr_lines[r][c]))
print(f"Changed cells: {len(changed)}")
from collections import defaultdict
by_row = defaultdict(list)
for r, c, old, new in changed:
    by_row[r].append((c, old, new))
for r in sorted(by_row):
    print(f"  row {r}: {by_row[r]}")

# Check new P Block 3
block3 = [curr_lines[r][36:41] for r in range(52, 57)]
print("\nNew P Block 3:")
for row in block3:
    print(f"  {row}")

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Top S patterns

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

<parameter=code>
import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Current P Block 3 after UP
p_block3 = [ascii_lines[r][36:41] for r in range(52, 57)]
print("Current P Block 3:")
for row in p_block3:
    print(f"  {row}")
print("Matches:", end=" ")
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == p_block3:
            print(f"topP[{j}] {name}", end=" ")
print()

# Check all current P blocks
positions = [15, 22, 29, 36, 43]
print("\nAll current P blocks:")
for i, c0 in enumerate(positions):
    block = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+5] for r in range(52, 57)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block:
                matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
    print(f"  Block {i}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")
    for row in block:
        print(f"    {row}")
    print()

# Current W markers are at block 3 (cols 36-40)
# Press DOWN to cycle P Block 3 back to P1
action(['DOWN'])
print("After DOWN on block 3:", last_action_result.get('board_changed'))

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')
block3 = [ascii_lines[r][36:41] for r in range(52, 57)]
print("P Block 3:")
for row in block3:
    print(f"  {row}")

# Check if it matches P1
import numpy as np
def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

matches = []
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == block3:
            matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
print(f"Matches: {matches}")

You are a coding agent solving a grid-based puzzle game.

Game overview:
- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.
- Re-ground on the newest frame after any score increase or abrupt scene change; the returned board may already be the next level.
- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.


Python tool guidance:
- Allowed imports: numpy (use it for board analysis--masks, unique, argwhere, diffs), bisect, collections, copy, fractions, functools, heapq, itertools, json, math, operator, random, re, statistics, string.
- Inspect `current_frame.segmentation`, `history`, and `valid_actions`; use ASCII only for a tiny crop. Print compact object lists, diffs, counts, coordinates, or local crops--never a full board or full animation frames.
- Every real action spends the level's step budget; checking a rule with `replay(step_fn)` against the transitions already recorded spends nothing. When a rule can be settled by the record, settle it there before spending actions on it.
- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

W markers are now at block 1 (cols 22-26). I need to cycle P Block 1 from P2 to P4. Let me figure out how many DOWN presses are needed. From the earlier observation, P Block 1 started at P5 rot90 and after 1 DOWN became P2 orig. I need to find how many more DOWN presses to get to P4.

<parameter=code>
import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Current P Block 1
block1 = [ascii_lines[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57)]
print("Current P Block 1:")
for row in block1:
    print(f"  {row}")

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Check matches
matches = []
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == block1:
            matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
print(f"Matches: {matches}")

# I need to cycle to P4. Let me press DOWN and check.
action(['DOWN'])
frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')
block1 = [ascii_lines[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57)]
print("\nAfter DOWN 1 on block 1:")
for row in block1:
    print(f"  {row}")
matches = []
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == block1:
            matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
print(f"Matches: {matches}")

Game overview:

Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!

- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Current P Block 1 after DOWN
block1 = [ascii_lines[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57)]
print("Current P Block 1:")
for row in block1:
    print(f"  {row}")
matches = []
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == block1:
            matches.append(f"topP[{j}] {name}")
print(f"Matches: {matches}")

# Check what topP[4] orig looks like
print("\ntopP[4] orig:")
for row in top_p[4]:
    print(f"  {row}")
print("topP[4] rot180:")
for row in rot_180(top_p[4]):
    print(f"  {row}")

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Current Block 1 is P3 rot90
# Let me press DOWN and record the sequence
for i in range(6):
    action(['DOWN'])
    frame = current_frame
    ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')
    block1 = [ascii_lines[r][22:27] for r in range(52, 57)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block1:
                matches.append(f"P{j} {name}")
    print(f"After DOWN {i+1}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")
    print(f"  Block: {block1}")

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Top S patterns
top_s = []

- You are solving a multi-level grid puzzle game. 
- You are called repeatedly over the course of a run. Treat each turn as one observe-plan-act cycle: re-understand the current state from the newest frame, update your working world model in Python, choose the next best action or short sequence against the goal as currently understood, execute it, and expect to re-evaluate on the next turn from the updated state.
- Your job is to solve the entire game by clearing every level, not just the current screen.
- Levels often build on earlier mechanics, but layouts and interactions can still change between levels.
- Optimize for as few in-game actions as possible while still being reliable.
- In this environment, boards are presented as 64 x 64 color grids rendered with ARC color symbols.
- Color legend: W=white, w=light gray, g=gray, G=dark gray, c=charcoal, B=black, M=magenta, P=pink, R=red, b=blue, S=sky blue, Y=yellow, O=orange, r=dark red, N=light green, p=purple.


Runtime variables inside every `python` tool call:
- `current_frame` is the latest board and exposes `.ascii`, `.segmentation`, `.step`, `.level`, and `.shape`; the raw numeric grid is unavailable.
- `current_frame.segmentation` returns `{'nodes': [...], 'adjacency_list': [...]}`. Each 4-connected same-color node has `id`, `color`, position-independent shape `hash`, `pixels`, clockwise `[row, col]` `boundary`, and enclosed-object `children`; adjacency pairs share an edge. Use this as the primary board view and `.ascii` only for a small local crop.
- `history` is a chronological list of objects with `.action` and post-action `.frame`; entries are not dicts. `history[-1].frame` equals `current_frame`, so use `previous_frame` or `history[-2].frame` for the prior board.
- `transitions` contains real actions with `.action`, `.before_frame`, `.after_frame`, `.frame`, and `.result`; `last_transition` is the latest one. Compare its before/after frames for settled diffs.
- `last_action_result` persists across inspection-only calls. Check `board_changed`, `reward`, `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, `run_complete`, and `valid_actions` after acting.
- `last_animation` is `None` before acting and otherwise describes only the most recent real action; historical transitions do not retain animation objects. Its primary fields are `.total_frames`, `.changed_frames`, `.outlier`, `.before_frame`, `.frames`, `.frame_indices`, and `.regions`. Never print full frames wholesale.
- An animation notice includes an automatic full-board storyboard when broad motion matters. Persistent local motion appears in `.regions`; print those summaries first, then inspect only a relevant area with `last_animation.region(i).inspect(rows=(r0,r1), cols=(c0,c1), max_frames=8)`. Inspection preserves aspect ratio and never aggregates source blocks larger than 8x8.
- `valid_actions` lists current action names. Execute with `action(['LEFT'])` or `action([{'action':'MOUSE','row':4,'col':7}])`; MOUSE uses integer `row`/`col`, never x/y.
- After `action(...)`, all runtime globals refresh before the next Python statement. One action may return multiple animation frames.


Visual-game guidance:
- Treat each board as a scene with objects, blockers, targets, adjacency, containment, motion, and symmetry.
- Game entities are usually be rendered as connected multi-tile shapes such as 2×2, 2×3, 3×3, or longer patterned structures. Sometime they might also be 1x1 tokens.- Some games are logic or layout puzzles with no explicit player avatar or controllable sprite on the board. Do not assume a player exists; the relevant state may be an object, region, cursor, selector, or whole-board configuration.
- Background colors are often white or gray/black-ish large regions, but not always. Verify background hypotheses by area, stability, and object boundaries rather than assuming them.
- In many games, a long horizontal or vertical line near an edge is a timer or remaining-steps bar. It often shrinks or changes each step. If you identify such a bar, do not get distracted by it or treat it as core gameplay state unless there is concrete evidence that it interacts with the puzzle mechanics.
A common failure mode is to mistake a segmented edge bar for clickable puzzle pieces. If a repeated strip of small blocks sits flush against the top, bottom, left, or right border and actions only change that strip while the interior board stays the same, classify it as HUD/timer state, not as an object to click through segment by segment. DON'T DO THIS!
- Use coordinates only to target actions or describe local evidence. Do not frame the objective as reaching a specific absolute row or column.

- `WIN` means the whole game is solved. Mid-run level completion is more likely to appear as a score increase while play continues.
- Strategies may transfer loosely across levels, but layouts and mechanics can change. Re-check the new board before repeating a plan.
- For `MOUSE`, pass `row` and `col` integer arguments. `row` is vertical position, `col` is horizontal position.

- Keep a compact world model: entities, action effects, likely goal, uncertainties, and shortest reliable plan. Probe only when evidence can distinguish hypotheses; once mechanics are understood, use a scorer, BFS/shortest-path search, or small action-sequence search.
- Default loop: summarize objects, infer the desired change, choose a probe or searched plan, execute it with `action(...)`, then check `last_action_result` and the refreshed board. Match objects using color, shape hash, overlap, proximity, area, and edge contact.
- After every action, distinguish gameplay change from a timer/progress-bar-only change. Stop immediately on `level_completed`, `done`, `game_over`, or `run_complete` and re-ground next turn.
- Animation monitoring warms up on the first five actions of each game. A novel broad tail may stop a queued sequence and automatically show a compact storyboard; similar tails continue with a reminder. Local persistent motion is summarized in `last_animation.regions`. Read the automatic storyboard first, and inspect a local crop only if its description could change the hypothesis.
- Use `print(...)` or `result` for short decision-oriented output. Call `action(...)` inside Python; batch a reliable sequence or call it repeatedly in a loop, checking refreshed state after each call.

# Block 2 is at P3 orig after 8 DOWNs. Need 3 more DOWNs to get to P0.
action(['DOWN'])
action(['DOWN'])
action(['DOWN'])

import numpy as np
frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

block2 = [ascii_lines[r][29:34] for r in range(52, 57)]
print("Block 2 after 3 more DOWNs:")
for row in block2:
    print(f"  {row}")
matches = []
for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
    for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
        if v == block2:
            matches.append(f"P{j} {name}")
print(f"Matches: {matches}")

# Move W markers from block 2 to block 4 (press RIGHT twice)
action(['RIGHT'])
action(['RIGHT'])

import numpy as np
frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Check W markers position
w_pos = []
for r in [48, 49]:
    for c in range(14, 49):
        if ascii_lines[r][c] == 'W':
            w_pos.append((r, c))
print(f"W markers: {w_pos}")

<parameter=code>
import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [22, 45]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Block 4 is at P0 orig. Press DOWN and observe.
for i in range(8):
    action(['DOWN'])
    frame = current_frame
    ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')
    block4 = [ascii_lines[r][43:48] for r in range(52, 57)]
    matches = []
    for j, p in enumerate(top_p):
        for name, v in [('orig', p), ('rot90', rot_cw(p)), ('rot180', rot_180(p)), ('rot270', rot_ccw(p))]:
            if v == block4:
                matches.append(f"P{j} {name}")
    print(f"After DOWN {i+1}: {matches if matches else 'NO MATCH'}")
</parameter>
</function>
</tool_call>

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top P patterns (3 rows x 2 cols)
top_p = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [5, 22]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_p.append(interior)

# Top Y patterns (3 rows x 2 cols)
top_y = []
for r0 in [4, 13, 22]:
    for c0 in [39, 56]:
        obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
        interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
        top_y.append(interior)

print("Top P patterns:")
for i, p in enumerate(top_p):
    print(f"  P{i}:")
    for row in p:
        print(f"    {row}")
    print()

print("Top Y patterns:")
for i, p in enumerate(top_y):
    print(f"  Y{i}:")
    for row in p:
        print(f"    {row}")
    print()

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Extract top Y blocks (7x7 each, interior 5x5)
# Row 1: Y at cols 38-58 (3 blocks)
# Row 2: Y at cols 45-58 (2 blocks)
# Row 3: Y at cols 52-58 (1 block)

top_y_blocks = []
# Row 1, block 1 (cols 38-44)
for r0, c0 in [(4, 38), (4, 45), (4, 52), (13, 45), (13, 52), (22, 52)]:
    obj = [ascii_lines[r][c0:c0+7] for r in range(r0, r0+7)]
    interior = [row[1:6] for row in obj[1:6]]
    top_y_blocks.append(interior)

for i, b in enumerate(top_y_blocks):

    print()

import numpy as np

frame = current_frame
ascii_lines = frame.ascii.split('\n')

def to_np_char(pattern):
    return np.array([list(row) for row in pattern])
def rot_cw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=-1).tolist()]
def rot_ccw(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=1).tolist()]
def rot_180(p):
    return [''.join(row) for row in np.rot90(to_np_char(p), k=2).tolist()]

# Top patterns
